Companies we look at: overview by product cluster

218 companies.

Legend

AI Control Framework

The framework describes what an AI evaluation and assurance product is sold for. It has five categories. Each company is scored from 0 to 3 per category by its focus: what the offering mainly consists of, not everything it offers. A score of 2 or 3 counts as high in that category; 0 and 1 do not.

  • Govern. Keeps a register of a company's AI systems and agents and sets what each may do: discovery or a register tied to rules, risk and regulation assessments recorded per system, approvals before a system or action goes ahead, and entitlement records for agent identity. It decides and records; it does not enforce at run time, and discovery alone scores 1.
  • Test. Probes an AI system with inputs the product creates or holds, separate from real user traffic, and scores the responses against criteria for quality, compliance or security: evaluation suites, red teaming, agent simulations, bias and regulation test packs. Static scans score 1.
  • Observe and prove. Turns what live systems actually did for real users into measurements, scores, reports and records: production traces with scoring, quality, cost and drift dashboards, reports mapped to a regulation. Storing logs only, or the product's own enforcement logs, scores 1.
  • Control and enforce. Sits in the live path and changes what happens before an action completes: it blocks, redacts, reroutes or holds the action for approval through a stated mechanism, including identity-aware checks and spend caps. Alerts and warn-only modes score 1.
  • Assure and insure. Gives people outside the AI's owner (customers, regulators, insurers, lenders) a result about the AI system that they can rely on, or moves the loss to a third party. It takes two forms: attestation (a score, report, audit opinion, certificate or trust mark issued by a party other than the one running the AI, including the testing behind another body's certificate and evaluations that a frontier lab publishes with the evaluator named) and risk transfer (insurance, an insured warranty or a guarantee; brokered placement). Reports that only the customer's own team reads, and benchmark scores nobody commissioned, do not count.

Depth scale.

  • 0, none: not offered.
  • 1, supporting: offered but not a main part: a feature, a beta or announced offer, logs or static scans only. For Assure and insure: evidence made for outsiders but issued by the company's own tool, or an offer with nothing bound or no independent assessor behind it.
  • 2, main part: a main part of the offering that meets the category description on the product's main paths.
  • 3, main part and leading: a main part and at least two of the category's cues. For Assure and insure the cues are: issued under accreditation or regulatory designation; a published standard or scheme with independent assessors; named parties that rely on the result (insurers, regulators, agencies); a public directory or registry of results; surveillance or re-assessment when the system changes; a bound policy or insured warranty; a named carrier or reinsurer that bears the loss.

Test, Observe and prove, and Control and enforce are treated as an evolution, so a company usually has one home among them and scores 1 on the earlier functions it offers as supporting features. Govern and Assure and insure are scored independently of them. Where two readers could disagree on how prominent a function is, the lower score is used.

Evaluating against

The four domains of what a product evaluates are a separate axis from the framework categories: they say what is checked, not where in the lifecycle.

  • Output quality: correct and useful outputs in ordinary use, measured as its own test type (accuracy, groundedness, task success).
  • Cost and performance: spend, tokens, latency and routing.
  • Security: attacks and abuse and the data exposure they cause: prompt injection, jailbreaks, leakage, tool and permission abuse, supply chain, rogue agents.
  • Behavior and compliance: conduct (outputs, decisions, behaviour) judged against a regulation, standard, fairness requirement or the customer's own content or conduct policy, with conformance reported. Security tests mapped to frameworks and attack-related content filters do not count.

Each domain is rated:

  • ✅ yes: a main part of the product evaluates the domain, with named criteria, checks or test types.
  • 🟡 partly: a supporting or limited check, a claim without criteria, or a separate named module that is not the main part.
  • ❌ no: not evaluated, or only an item in a risk list.

Arize AIBerkeley, United States
2020		Mature
Now a unit of public company Dynatrace, acquired for about US$915M in October 2026 after roughly US$131M of venture funding.		Horizontal

0Govern: Not offered.

2Test: Learn runs prompt and harness experiments and offline evals on datasets before deploying; red teaming is only a secondary feature.

3Observe and prove: Observe traces agents; Evaluate and Signal score production traces with LLM judges and group recurring failures into ranked issues with cost tracking.

0Control and enforce: Not offered.

0Assure and insure: Not offered: evals and traces are read by the customer's AI engineers. Trust center shows Arize's own SOC 2, ISO 27001, PCI, HIPAA; no AIUC-1 or ISO 42001 assessor role, attestation product or insurer link.

✅ Output quality🟡 Cost and performance🟡 Security❌ Behavior and compliance

Arize evaluates agent outputs and behaviour on the customer's production traces (spans, traces, sessions) and on datasets and experiments before release. Criteria are customer-written or built-in templates scored by LLM judges, code checks or human annotation, such as correctness, hallucination and agent trajectory quality.

  • Production quality scoring: online evals on spans, traces and sessions with LLM judges, code checks, agent-as-judge, Decision Judge or human review; example criteria are helpfulness, tone, correctness, hallucination and trajectory.
  • Pre-release experiments: offline evals on datasets and experiments, run in CI/CD, and prompt comparison in a playground before deploying.
  • LLM red teaming: adversarial prompt datasets for prompt injection, harmful content, data privacy and misinformation, scored before deployment (docs); cost per trace is tracked separately.

3Test: LLM-as-a-judge evaluation with 30+ metrics and Test Suites with pass or fail assertions on test items, run in development or CI.

3Observe and prove: Production monitoring scores live traces, alerts on failed criteria and tracks token and cost; Cost Intelligence tracks coding-agent spend.

1Control and enforce: Supporting: guardrails block PII and policy violations through the SDK, but sit under production monitoring and are not a separate product.

0Assure and insure: Not offered: evaluation, tracing and guardrails for the customer's own developers. No certificate, outsider report, AIUC-1 role or insurer link on its pages or in search.

✅ Output quality🟡 Cost and performance🟡 Security❌ Behavior and compliance

Evaluates LLM application and agent outputs on traces from development, testing and production and on reference datasets, using LLM judges and heuristic metrics for relevance, hallucination and task completion, plus customer-written pass/fail assertions. Also tracks token and model cost and applies guardrails for PII and policy violations.

  • Test Suite assertions: natural-language rules (for example "the response does not hallucinate") checked by an LLM judge, at suite and item level, with execution policies on pass counts.
  • Dataset and metric scoring: 30+ pre-built and custom metrics (hallucination, answer relevance, context precision, task completion) in experiments compared across versions.
  • Production trace scoring and guardrails: online evaluators (LLM judge, decision model or code) score live traces with alerts; five guard types (PII, topic, prompt injection, LLM judge, custom classifier) block in the live path.
Judgment LabsSan Francisco, United States
2025		Well-funded Early Stage
Founded about 2025 (sources differ) and raised US$32M across seed and Series A, led by Lightspeed, in May 2026.		Horizontal

0Govern: Not offered.

2Test: Tests run agents on suites of cases scored by the same judges, gate pull requests in CI and compare models or prompts.

3Observe and prove: Monitoring judges label live traces and sessions, Issues groups failures by root cause, and Slack or email alerts fire on labels.

0Control and enforce: Not offered.

0Assure and insure: Not offered: monitoring judges and issue discovery for the customer's own team; SOC 2 Type II and HIPAA are the company's own; no outward-facing attestation, certificate or insurer link found.

✅ Output quality🟡 Cost and performance❌ Security❌ Behavior and compliance

Judgment evaluates the traces of a customer's production agents with customer-defined judges, and groups recurring failures it discovers on its own. The same judges score agent runs on offline test suites of customer-built cases. Criteria are rubrics, conditions and code written by the customer.

  • Production quality scoring: agentic judges apply customer rubrics and labels to live traces and sessions; condition judges check duration, LLM cost and errors; custom code judges; judges can be aligned to human labels.
  • Issue discovery: a background agent reads recent traces and reports recurring failures by root cause, severity and evidence.
  • Offline test suites: cases with expected judge answers run on a dataset to gate pull requests and compare models or prompts.

✅ Output quality🟡 Cost and performance❌ Security❌ Behavior and compliance

Laminar scores agent behaviour on traces of customer agent runs and on fixed datasets. Signals applies a trace-analysis model to find failures against plain-language instructions the customer writes. Offline evaluations score outputs with customer-written functions. Tokens and cost are tracked per run. No regulations or standards are named.

  • Production failure detection: Signals reads agent traces against a customer instruction and JSON schema, for example unsupported claims or wrong-document citations, clusters matches and alerts; criteria are customer-defined.
  • Offline evaluations: runs prompts, pipelines or agents on fixed datasets, scores each output with customer functions and compares runs for regressions; agent evaluations score a whole run trajectory.
  • Cost tracking: records tokens and cost per call, with custom model prices and a cost view per trace.

Evaluates real production sessions of the customer's agents, scoring each trace with built-in detectors and customer-defined ones. Criteria are behaviour categories (refusal, frustration, jailbreaking, PII leakage, tool errors) and customer-written rules; no regulations or standards named.

  • Production quality scoring: built-in flaggers score live traces for refusal, frustration, bluffing, tool-call errors, empty responses and schema failures; LLM-based flaggers sample about 10% of sessions by default.
  • Security and privacy flags: flaggers mark jailbreaking, NSFW content and PII leakage in production traces; detector models and thresholds not published.
  • Customer-defined signals: a plain-language behaviour or rule becomes an LLM-judge, condition or script detector, tested on recent sessions, then run on every matching session.

🟡 Output quality🟡 Cost and performance❌ Security❌ Behavior and compliance

Raindrop evaluates how production agents behave, using the customer's live traces and, in Simulations, replayed production traffic and test cases. It scores deviations from forecast normal for tool errors, latency and user signals, and judges whether a fix improves behaviour. Criteria are learned from traffic or defined by the team as Signals.

  • Production issue detection: anomaly detection on tool error rate, latency and signals, with severity from user impact; issues carry a confidence score; underlying model not published.
  • Signals and experiments: customer-defined behaviours, such as user frustration or a success claim with no tool result, scored on production events by a classifier using tool-call code and semantic reasoning; versions compared.
  • Simulations (early access): replays production traffic and existing test cases against a change on each pull request and applies anomaly detection to the results.

✅ Output quality🟡 Cost and performance❌ Security❌ Behavior and compliance

DataFramer evaluates AI in customers' production traces and workflows, plus human expert reviews. It scores accuracy with reviewer grades and calibrated LLM judges against rubrics the customer writes, tracks judge and human agreement, and links AI quality to defined business outcomes such as cycle time.

  • Production quality scoring: Human Score, AI Judge Score and Judge-Human Alignment are tracked across traces; Findings states over 82% accuracy at surfacing real issues.
  • Expert rubric review: Customer-written rubrics with scored dimensions guide reviewers; a reviewer-agreement score shows if the standard is clear; corrections become regression tests.
  • Trace issue detection: Findings looks for hallucinations, ignored retrieval, failed tool calls, expensive or slow traces, prompt injection and policy violations; criteria are described in plain language by the user.

  • Production quality scoring: server-side evaluators score sampled live traces for groundedness, answer faithfulness, tool correctness and toxicity against customer-set thresholds, with alerts on drift.
  • Offline regression testing: experiments compare agent versions on datasets built from production failures; automated evals in CI block releases that regress.

2Observe and prove: Measure Risk: signs and timestamps each agent action, approval and policy decision, and computes a 0 to 100 risk score from that record (score endpoints in beta).

3Control and enforce: Control Risk: checks each action against deterministic limits before it runs, then allows, blocks or escalates to a human for OTP or passkey approval.

2Assure and insure: Its main commercial line since the pivot: a 0 to 100 agent risk score and signed receipts sold as Risk Intelligence to insurers, lenders and auditors; nobody is shown paying yet and no certificate or carrier is named.

❌ Output quality❌ Cost and performance🟡 Security🟡 Behavior and compliance

Humanos scores the risk of AI agents that act through its mandates: verified identity, exposure and compliance with the rules set for each agent, with each action, payment and approval kept as signed evidence. It tests no model and runs no evaluations or red teaming.

  • Compliance with agent rules: each tool call is checked against the agent's policy (allow, restrict, delegate, deny), input rules and signed mandate limits before it runs, then allowed, blocked or escalated; conformance feeds the score. No external framework.
  • Runtime risk scoring: a 0 to 100 score from identity strength, compliance behaviour and exposure, times an anomaly term set to 1 until the live loop exists; formula in the API documentation; beta.
  • Verifiable audit evidence: signed, timestamped, tamper-evident records support audit and underwriting; W3C Verifiable Credentials are the format and SOC 2 Type II is in audit.

Munich Re aiSureMunich, Germany
2018 (first AI policy)		Mature
A unit of the listed reinsurer Munich Re, with about 43,982 staff in the group (December 2025); the unit's own size is not published.		Horizontal

0Govern: Not offered.

1Test: Munich Re's data scientists review the provider's data process and test procedure and analyse its inputs and outputs statistically; no sign that they generate probing inputs of their own.

1Observe and prove: Settlement rests on production performance measured against ground truth that the provider supplies from locked predictions; who runs the monitoring (provider or Munich Re) is not stated.

0Control and enforce: Not offered.

3Assure and insure: Insurance cover and insurance-backed performance warranties for AI systems, with the loss borne by Munich Re and partner insurers (Great Lakes Insurance SE, Mosaic, up to 15M capacity; the currency differs by outlet); the premium signals model quality.

✅ Output quality❌ Cost and performance❌ Security❌ Behavior and compliance

Munich Re's data scientists review how the AI model is built and tested, then estimate from the provider's historical performance data how likely it is to underperform. The provider and Munich Re agree where the model is valid and which performance metric counts; the guarantee threshold comes from that metric's probability distribution, and the payout grows with the shortfall. The model's quality and stability set the premium, and the provider keeps part of the risk through coinsurance. Claims are settled on measured performance against the threshold, with the provider locking predictions and supplying ground truth.

  • Who evaluates: Munich Re's own data scientists and research team do the review, with domain experts added case by case, for example a cyber team for a malware model.
  • What is checked: data generation and annotation, training and testing procedure, retraining, drift risk and input and output statistics.
  • Method: the risk estimator uses conformal prediction on historical performance, and a 2023 paper co-authored by Munich Re's Michael Berger shows its failure probabilities to be conservative.
  • Terms effect: premium follows model quality and stability, the threshold follows the metric's distribution, and exclusions, deductibles and limits apply.
  • Trigger and limits: a parametric-like payout on a pre-agreed measurable threshold with no negligence needed; discrimination and fines are covered losses but no test for them was found, and malicious attacks are described as outside the performance product.
